  data drift, and periodically retraining the model on fresh data.
\item
  Security: How will the model and its predictions be protected from
  abuse or unauthorized access? This may involve techniques like input
  validation, output filtering, or access controls.
\end{itemize}

Deploying and maintaining machine learning models in production is a
complex topic that requires close collaboration between data scientists,
software engineers, and domain experts. It's an active area of research
and development, with new tools and best practices emerging regularly.

To bring everything together, let's return one last time to our housing
price prediction example. After training and validating our regularized
linear regression model, we can evaluate its performance on the test
set:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ sklearn.metrics }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ mean\_squared\_error, mean\_absolute\_error, r2\_score}

\CommentTok{\# Generate predictions on the test set}
\NormalTok{y\_pred }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.dot(X\_test, w)}

\CommentTok{\# Calculate evaluation metrics}
\NormalTok{mse }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mean\_squared\_error(y\_test, y\_pred)}
\NormalTok{mae }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mean\_absolute\_error(y\_test, y\_pred)}
\NormalTok{r2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ r2\_score(y\_test, y\_pred)}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Test MSE: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{mse}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Test MAE: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{mae}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Test R\^{}2: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{r2}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If we're satisfied with the model's performance, we can deploy it as
part of a larger housing price estimation service. This might involve:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Wrapping the trained model in a web service API that can accept new
  housing features and return price predictions.
\item
  Integrating the API with a user-facing application that allows
  homeowners or real estate agents to input property information and
  receive estimates.
\item
  Setting up a data pipeline to continuously collect new housing data
  and periodically retrain the model to capture changing market
  conditions.
\item
  Defining monitoring dashboards and alerts to track the model's
  performance over time and detect any anomalies or degradations.
\item
  Establishing governance policies and processes for managing the
  lifecycle of the model, from development to retirement.
\end{itemize}

Again, this is a simplified example, but it illustrates the end-to-end
process of building a machine learning system, from data preparation to
model development to deployment and maintenance.

\section{The Ethics and Governance of Machine
Learning}\label{the-ethics-and-governance-of-machine-learning}

As machine learning systems become more prevalent and powerful, it's
crucial that we grapple with the ethical implications of their
development and deployment. In this final section, we'll explore some
key ethical considerations and governance principles for responsible
machine learning.

\subsection{Fairness and Bias}\label{fairness-and-bias}

One of the most pressing ethical challenges in machine learning is
ensuring that models are fair and unbiased. If the training data
reflects historical biases or discrimination, the resulting model may
perpetuate or even amplify those biases in its predictions.

For example, consider a hiring model that is trained on past hiring
decisions to predict the likelihood of a candidate being successful in a
job. If the training data comes from a company with a history of
discriminatory hiring practices, the model may learn to penalize
candidates from underrepresented groups, even if those factors are not
actually predictive of job performance.

Detecting and mitigating bias in machine learning systems is an active
area of research, with techniques like:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Demographically balancing datasets to ensure equal representation of
  different groups
\item
  Adversarial debiasing to remove sensitive information from model
  representations
\item
  Regularization techniques to penalize models that exhibit disparate
  impact
\item
  Post-processing methods to adjust model outputs to satisfy fairness
  constraints
\end{itemize}

However, these techniques are not perfect, and there is often a tradeoff
between fairness and accuracy. Moreover, fairness is not a purely
technical issue, but a sociotechnical one that requires ongoing
collaboration between machine learning practitioners, domain experts,
policymakers, and affected communities.

\subsection{Transparency and
Accountability}\label{transparency-and-accountability}

Another key ethical principle for machine learning is transparency and
accountability. As models become more complex and consequential, it
becomes harder for humans to understand how they arrive at their
predictions and to trace the provenance of their training data and
design choices.

This opacity can make it difficult to audit models for bias, safety, or
compliance with regulations. It can also make it harder to challenge or
appeal decisions made by machine learning systems, leading to a loss of
human agency and recourse.

Some techniques for promoting transparency and accountability in machine
learning include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Model interpretability methods that provide human-understandable
  explanations of model predictions
\item
  Provenance tracking to document the lineage of data, code, and models
  used in a system
\item
  Audit trails and version control to enable reproducibility and
  historical analysis
\item
  Participatory design processes that involve affected stakeholders in
  the development and governance of models
\end{itemize}

However, like fairness, transparency is not purely a technical problem.
It also requires institutional structures and processes for oversight,
redress, and accountability. This might involve things like:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Designating responsible individuals or teams for the ethical
  development and deployment of machine learning systems
\item
  Establishing review boards or oversight committees to assess the
  social impact and governance of models
\item
  Creating channels for affected individuals and communities to provide
  input and feedback on the use of machine learning in their lives
\item
  Developing legal and regulatory frameworks to enforce transparency and
  accountability standards
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Safety and Robustness}\label{safety-and-robustness}

As machine learning systems are deployed in increasingly high-stakes
domains, from healthcare to transportation to criminal justice, ensuring
their safety and robustness becomes paramount. Models that are brittle,
unreliable, or easily fooled can lead to serious harms if they are not
carefully designed and tested.

Some key challenges in machine learning safety and robustness include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Distributional shift, where models trained on one data distribution
  may fail unexpectedly when applied to a different distribution
\item
  Adversarial attacks, where malicious actors can craft inputs that fool
  models into making egregious errors
\item
  Reward hacking, where optimizing for the wrong objective function can
  lead models to behave in unintended and harmful ways
\item
  Safe exploration, where models need to learn about their environment
  without taking catastrophic actions
\end{itemize}

Techniques for improving the safety and robustness of machine learning
systems include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Anomaly and out-of-distribution detection to flag inputs that are far
  from the training data
\item
  Adversarial training and robustness regularization to make models more
  resilient to perturbations
\item
  Constrained optimization and safe reinforcement learning to respect
  safety boundaries during learning
\item
  Formal verification and testing to provide guarantees about model
  behavior under different conditions
\end{itemize}

However, building truly safe and robust machine learning systems
requires more than just technical solutions. It also requires:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Rigorous safety culture and practices throughout the development and
  deployment lifecycle
\item
  Close collaboration between machine learning practitioners, domain
  experts, and safety professionals
\item
  Proactive engagement with policymakers and the public to align the
  development of machine learning with societal values and expectations
\item
  Ongoing monitoring and adjustment of deployed systems to catch and
  correct errors and unintended consequences
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Ethical Principles and Governance
Frameworks}\label{ethical-principles-and-governance-frameworks}

\begin{center}
\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{contents/core/img/chapter10_responsibleai.png}}
\end{center}

To navigate the complex ethical landscape of machine learning, we need
clear principles and governance frameworks to guide responsible
development and deployment. Some key principles that have been proposed
include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Transparency}: Machine learning systems should be auditable
  and understandable by humans.
\item
  \textbf{Accountability}: There should be clear mechanisms for
  oversight, redress, and enforcement.
\item
  \textbf{Fairness}: Machine learning should treat all individuals
  equitably and avoid discriminatory impacts.
\item
  \textbf{Safety}: Machine learning systems should be reliable, robust,
  and safe throughout their lifecycle.
\item
  \textbf{Privacy}: The collection and use of data for machine learning
  should respect individual privacy rights and provide appropriate
  protections.
\item
  \textbf{Human agency}: Machine learning systems should respect human
  autonomy and dignity, and provide meaningful opportunities for human
  input and control.
\item
  \textbf{Societal benefit}: The development and deployment of machine
  learning should be guided by considerations of social good and
  collective wellbeing.
\end{itemize}

Translating these high-level principles into practical governance
frameworks is an ongoing challenge, but some key elements include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Ethical codes of conduct and professional standards for machine
  learning practitioners
\item
  Impact assessment and risk management processes to identify and
  mitigate potential harms
\item
  Stakeholder engagement and participatory design to ensure affected
  communities have a voice
\item
  Regulatory sandboxes and policy experiments to test new governance
  approaches
\item
  International standards and coordination to address the global nature
  of machine learning development
\end{itemize}

Ultimately, the goal of machine learning governance should be to ensure
that the technology is developed and deployed in a way that aligns with
human values and enhances, rather than undermines, human flourishing.
This is a complex and ongoing process that will require sustained
collaboration across disciplines, sectors, and geographies.

\section{Conclusion}\label{conclusion}

In this chapter, we've embarked on a comprehensive exploration of the
foundations of machine learning, from its historical roots to its modern
techniques to its future challenges. We've seen how the field has
evolved from rule-based expert systems to data-driven statistical
learning, powered by the explosion of big data and computing power.
We've examined the fundamental components of machine learning systems -
the data they learn from, the patterns they aim to extract, and the
algorithms that power the learning process. We've discussed key concepts
like inductive bias, generalization, overfitting, and regularization,
and how they relate to the art of building effective models. We've
walked through the practical steps of constructing a machine learning
pipeline, from data preparation to model selection to deployment and
monitoring. And we've grappled with some of the ethical challenges and
governance principles that arise when building systems that can have
significant impact on people's lives.

The field of machine learning is still rapidly evolving, with new
breakthroughs and challenges emerging every year. As we look to the
future, some of the key frontiers and open questions include:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \emph{Continual and lifelong learning}: How can we build models that
  can learn continuously and adapt to new tasks and domains over time,
  without forgetting what they've learned before?
\item
  \emph{Causality and interpretability}: How can we move beyond purely
  associational patterns to uncover causal relationships and build
  models that are more interpretable and explainable to humans?
\item
  \emph{Robustness and safety}: How can we guarantee that machine
  learning systems will behave safely and reliably, even in the face of
  distributional shift, adversarial attacks, or unexpected situations?
\item
  \emph{Human-AI collaboration}: How can we design machine learning
  systems that augment and empower human intelligence, rather than
  replacing or undermining it?
\item
  \emph{Ethical alignment}: How can we ensure that the development and
  deployment of machine learning aligns with human values and promotes
  beneficial outcomes for society as a whole?
\end{itemize}

Advancing machine learning requires collaboration across
disciplines---from computer science and statistics to psychology, social
science, philosophy, and ethics. It also demands engagement with
policymakers, industry leaders, and the public to ensure responsible and
inclusive development. The goal is to build systems that learn from
experience to make decisions that benefit humanity, whether in
healthcare, scientific discovery, or improving daily life.

However, realizing this potential goes beyond technical progress; it
requires addressing fairness, accountability, transparency, and safety
while navigating ethical and governance challenges. Machine learning
practitioners must not only push technological boundaries but also
consider the broader impact of their work. By embracing diverse
perspectives and collaborating beyond our field, we shape the future of
AI. Staying curious, critical, and committed to responsible development
will ensure machine learning serves society for generations to come.

\chapter{The ``Hello World'' of TinyML}\label{the-hello-world-of-tinyml}

\section{Introduction to Microcontroller-Based Machine
Learning}\label{introduction-to-microcontroller-based-machine-learning}

Machine learning at the edge represents a significant paradigm shift in
computational intelligence, enabling sophisticated inferencing
capabilities on resource-constrained embedded systems such as the
EFR32MG24 Wireless Gecko microcontroller. This chapter explores the
theoretical foundations and practical implementations of TinyML
specifically tailored for microcontroller deployment, with particular
focus on the sine wave prediction model as the canonical ``Hello World''
example of TinyML.

The concept of a ``Hello World'' example has long been a tradition in
programming, where new technologies are introduced with simple code that
demonstrates basic functionality. In the domain of TinyML, our sine wave
prediction serves as an elegant introduction to the end-to-end process
of building, training, and deploying models to microcontrollers.

\section{Theoretical Foundations of TinyML for
Microcontrollers}\label{theoretical-foundations-of-tinyml-for-microcontrollers}

\subsection{The Computational Constraints
Paradigm}\label{the-computational-constraints-paradigm}

Traditional machine learning systems operate under the assumption of
abundant computational resources, where model complexity and size are
secondary concerns to performance metrics. TinyML, however, inverts this
paradigm, placing primary emphasis on resource efficiency while
maintaining acceptable inferencing quality.

For the EFR32MG24 platform, with its ARM Cortex-M33 core, limited memory
footprint (1536KB flash and 256KB RAM), and power-sensitive
applications, we must consider:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Memory-Constrained Learning}: Operating within a 256KB RAM
  budget necessitates models with minimal memory footprints
\item
  \textbf{Computation-Constrained Inference}: The 78MHz Cortex-M33
  processor requires algorithmic optimizations to achieve real-time
  performance
\item
  \textbf{Energy-Constrained Execution}: Battery-powered applications
  demand power-aware ML implementations
\end{enumerate}

These constraints fundamentally reshape our approach to machine learning
model design, training methodologies, and deployment strategies.

\subsection{Model Compression and
Quantization}\label{model-compression-and-quantization}

Central to TinyML is the concept of model compression, which can be
formalized as an optimization problem:

\[\min_{\theta'} \mathcal{L}(f_{\theta'}(X), Y) \quad \text{s.t.} \quad |\theta'| \ll |\theta|\]

Where \(\theta\) represents the parameters of the original model,
\(\theta'\) the compressed model parameters, \(\mathcal{L}\) the loss
function, and \(f_{\theta'}(X)\) the model predictions on input \(X\)
compared against ground truth \(Y\).

Quantization---a key technique in this domain---transforms
floating-point weights and activations to reduced-precision integers:

\[Q(w) = \text{round}\left(\frac{w}{\Delta}\right) \cdot \Delta\]

Where \(\Delta\) represents the quantization step size. This
transformation reduces both memory requirements and computational
complexity at the cost of some precision.

\section{Building Our Sine Wave Model in Google
Colab}\label{building-our-sine-wave-model-in-google-colab}

\subsection{Generating and Processing the
Dataset}\label{generating-and-processing-the-dataset}

For our introductory TinyML example, we'll create a sine wave predictor
that learns to approximate the sine function. This represents an ideal
starting point for several reasons:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The sine function is mathematically well-defined and bounded
\item
  The input-output relationship exhibits nonlinearity that requires
  proper model architecture
\item
  The implementation can produce visually verifiable results on a
  microcontroller
\end{enumerate}

Let's begin by creating a Google Colab notebook to build and train our
model. Open a new notebook and start with the following code to generate
our training data:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ tensorflow }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ tf}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tensorflow.keras }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ layers}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ os}

\CommentTok{\# Generate a uniformly distributed set of random numbers in the range from}
\CommentTok{\# 0 to 2π, which covers a complete sine wave oscillation}
\NormalTok{SAMPLES }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1000}
\NormalTok{np.random.seed(}\DecValTok{1337}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_values }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.random.uniform(low}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, high}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{math.pi, size}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{SAMPLES)}
\CommentTok{\# Shuffle the values to guarantee they\textquotesingle{}re not in order}
\NormalTok{np.random.shuffle(x\_values)}
\CommentTok{\# Calculate the corresponding sine values}
\NormalTok{y\_values }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sin(x\_values)}

\CommentTok{\# Add a small random number to each y value to simulate noise}
\NormalTok{y\_values }\OperatorTok{+=} \FloatTok{0.1} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ np.random.randn(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{y\_values.shape)}

\CommentTok{\# Split into train/validation/test sets}
\NormalTok{TRAIN\_SPLIT }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.6} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ SAMPLES)}
\NormalTok{TEST\_SPLIT }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ SAMPLES }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ TRAIN\_SPLIT)}
\NormalTok{x\_train, x\_validate, x\_test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.split(x\_values, [TRAIN\_SPLIT, TEST\_SPLIT])}
\NormalTok{y\_train, y\_validate, y\_test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.split(y\_values, [TRAIN\_SPLIT, TEST\_SPLIT])}

\CommentTok{\# Plot our data points}
\NormalTok{plt.figure(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{plt.scatter(x\_train, y\_train, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Training data\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.scatter(x\_validate, y\_validate, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Validation data\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.scatter(x\_test, y\_test, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Test data\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.title(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Sine Wave with Random Noise\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.xlabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}x values\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.ylabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}y values (sine of x + noise)\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Constructing and Training the Neural Network
Model}\label{constructing-and-training-the-neural-network-model}

Now we'll construct a simple neural network to learn the sine function:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Create a model with 2 layers of 16 neurons each}
\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.keras.Sequential()}
\CommentTok{\# First layer takes a scalar input and feeds it through 16 "neurons"}
\NormalTok{model.add(layers.Dense(}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, input\_shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,)))}
\CommentTok{\# Second layer with 16 neurons to capture non{-}linear relationships}
\NormalTok{model.add(layers.Dense(}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{))}
\CommentTok{\# Final layer is a single neuron for our output value}
\NormalTok{model.add(layers.Dense(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\CommentTok{\# Compile the model using a standard optimizer and loss function for regression}
\NormalTok{model.}\BuiltInTok{compile}\NormalTok{(optimizer}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rmsprop\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, loss}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mse\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, metrics}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mae\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{])}

\CommentTok{\# Display model summary to understand its structure}
\NormalTok{model.summary()}

\CommentTok{\# Train the model on our data}
\NormalTok{history }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model.fit(x\_train, y\_train, }
\NormalTok{                    epochs}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{, }
\NormalTok{                    batch\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                    validation\_data}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(x\_validate, y\_validate),}
\NormalTok{                    verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Plot the training and validation loss}
\NormalTok{plt.figure(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(history.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(history.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}val\_loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Validation Loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.title(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Training and Validation Loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.xlabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Epoch\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.ylabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}

\CommentTok{\# Plot the training and validation mean absolute error}
\NormalTok{plt.figure(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(history.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mae\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}MAE\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(history.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}val\_mae\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Validation MAE\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.title(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Training and Validation Mean Absolute Error\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.xlabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Epoch\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.ylabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}MAE\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}

\CommentTok{\# Evaluate the model on our test data}
\NormalTok{test\_loss, test\_mae }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model.evaluate(x\_test, y\_test)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}Test Loss: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{test\_loss}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f\textquotesingle{}Test MAE: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{test\_mae}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Generate predictions across the full range for visualization}
\NormalTok{x\_dense }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{math.pi, }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y\_dense\_true }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sin(x\_dense)}
\NormalTok{y\_dense\_pred }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model.predict(x\_dense)}

\CommentTok{\# Plot the true sine curve against our model\textquotesingle{}s predictions}
\NormalTok{plt.figure(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(x\_dense, y\_dense\_true, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}b{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}True Sine\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.plot(x\_dense, y\_dense\_pred, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Model Prediction\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.scatter(x\_test, y\_test, alpha}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{, label}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Test Data\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.title(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Sine Wave Prediction\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.xlabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}x\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.ylabel(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sin(x)\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This architecture, though simple, is carefully designed to capture the
nonlinear relationship of the sine function. The ReLU (Rectified Linear
Unit) activation function is particularly important as it introduces
nonlinearity:

\[\text{ReLU}(x) = \max(0, x)\]

We train the model using the mean squared error loss function, which for
a regression problem is defined as:

\[\text{MSE} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2\]

Where \(y_i\) represents the actual sine value and \(\hat{y}_i\)
represents our model's prediction.

\section{Optimizing for Microcontroller
Deployment}\label{optimizing-for-microcontroller-deployment}

\subsection{Model Conversion and Quantization for TensorFlow
Lite}\label{model-conversion-and-quantization-for-tensorflow-lite}

To deploy our trained model to the EFR32MG24 microcontroller, we must
convert it into a format suitable for resource-constrained devices:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Convert the model to the TensorFlow Lite format without quantization}
\NormalTok{converter }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.lite.TFLiteConverter.from\_keras\_model(model)}
\NormalTok{tflite\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ converter.convert()}

\CommentTok{\# Save the model to disk}
\ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"sine\_model.tflite"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"wb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{    f.write(tflite\_model)}

\CommentTok{\# Convert with quantization for further optimization}
\NormalTok{converter }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.lite.TFLiteConverter.from\_keras\_model(model)}
\NormalTok{converter.optimizations }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [tf.lite.Optimize.DEFAULT]}

\CommentTok{\# Define a generator function that provides our test data\textquotesingle{}s x values}
\CommentTok{\# as a representative dataset}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ representative\_dataset\_generator():}
  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ value }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ x\_test:}
    \ControlFlowTok{yield}\NormalTok{ [np.array(value, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32, ndmin}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)]}
    
\NormalTok{converter.representative\_dataset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ representative\_dataset\_generator}
\NormalTok{tflite\_model\_quantized }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ converter.convert()}

\CommentTok{\# Save the quantized model to disk}
\ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"sine\_model\_quantized.tflite"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"wb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{    f.write(tflite\_model\_quantized)}

\CommentTok{\# Print the size reduction achieved through quantization}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Original model size: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tflite\_model)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ bytes"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Quantized model size: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tflite\_model\_quantized)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ bytes"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Size reduction: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tflite\_model\_quantized) }\OperatorTok{/} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tflite\_model)) }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{100}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\%"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Converting to C Code for Embedded
Systems}\label{converting-to-c-code-for-embedded-systems}

For deployment on microcontrollers like the EFR32MG24, we need to
convert our quantized model into a C header file that can be directly
included in our firmware:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Function to convert the model to a C array}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ convert\_tflite\_to\_c\_array(tflite\_model, array\_name):}
\NormalTok{    hex\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\StringTok{"0x}\SpecialCharTok{\{:02x\}}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{.}\BuiltInTok{format}\NormalTok{(byte) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ byte }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ tflite\_model]}
\NormalTok{    c\_array }\OperatorTok{=} \SpecialStringTok{f"const unsigned char }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{array\_name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{[] = }\CharTok{\{\{\textbackslash{}n}\SpecialStringTok{"}
    
    \CommentTok{\# Format the hex data into rows}
\NormalTok{    chunk\_size }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{12}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(hex\_data), chunk\_size):}
\NormalTok{        c\_array }\OperatorTok{+=} \StringTok{"  "} \OperatorTok{+} \StringTok{", "}\NormalTok{.join(hex\_data[i:i}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{chunk\_size]) }\OperatorTok{+} \StringTok{",}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}
    
\NormalTok{    c\_array }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ c\_array[:}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+} \StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{\};}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}
\NormalTok{    c\_array }\OperatorTok{+=} \SpecialStringTok{f"const unsigned int }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{array\_name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{\_len = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tflite\_model)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{;}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\SpecialStringTok{"}
    
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ c\_array}

\CommentTok{\# Generate the C array for our model}
\NormalTok{c\_array }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ convert\_tflite\_to\_c\_array(tflite\_model\_quantized, }\StringTok{"g\_sine\_model\_data"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Save to a header file}
\ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"sine\_model\_data.h"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"w"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{    f.write(}\StringTok{"\#ifndef SINE\_MODEL\_DATA\_H\_}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    f.write(}\StringTok{"\#define SINE\_MODEL\_DATA\_H\_}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    f.write(}\StringTok{"\#include \textless{}stdint.h\textgreater{}}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    f.write(c\_array)}
\NormalTok{    f.write(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{\#endif  // SINE\_MODEL\_DATA\_H\_}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"C header file generated: sine\_model\_data.h"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Download the files}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ google.colab }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ files}
\NormalTok{files.download(}\StringTok{"sine\_model.tflite"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{files.download(}\StringTok{"sine\_model\_quantized.tflite"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{files.download(}\StringTok{"sine\_model\_data.h"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Deploying with Simplicity Studio and Gecko
SDK}\label{deploying-with-simplicity-studio-and-gecko-sdk}

Now that we have our trained model in a format suitable for
microcontrollers, we'll implement the TinyML application using
Simplicity Studio and the Gecko SDK. This approach simplifies
development by providing a structured framework for EFR32 devices.

\subsection{Creating a New Project in Simplicity
Studio}\label{creating-a-new-project-in-simplicity-studio}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Launch Simplicity Studio and connect your EFR32MG24 development board
\item
  Select your device in the ``Debug Adapters'' view
\item
  Click on ``Create New Project'' in the Launcher perspective
\item
  Select ``Silicon Labs Project Wizard'' and click ``Next''
\item
  Choose ``Gecko SDK'' as the project type
\item
  Filter for ``example'' and select ``TensorFlow Lite Micro Example''
  template
\item
  Configure project settings:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Name: \texttt{sine\_wave\_predictor}
  \item
    SDK version: Latest version
  \item
    Click ``Next'' and then ``Finish''
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Project Structure and Important
Files}\label{project-structure-and-important-files}

Simplicity Studio creates a project with the following important files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{app.c}: Main application entry point
\item
  \textbf{sl\_tflite\_micro\_model.\{h,c\}}: TensorFlow Lite Micro
  integration
\item
  \textbf{sl\_pwm.\{h,c\}}: PWM control for LED output
\item
  \textbf{sine\_model\_data.h}: Our model data (to be replaced with our
  trained model)
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Adding Our Trained Model}\label{adding-our-trained-model}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  In Simplicity Studio, locate the project's \texttt{inc} folder
\item
  Right-click and select ``Import'' → ``General'' → ``File System''
\item
  Browse to the location where you saved \texttt{sine\_model\_data.h}
\item
  Select the file and click ``Finish''
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Implementing the Application
Logic}\label{implementing-the-application-logic}

Now we'll modify the application code to use our sine wave model. Open
\texttt{app.c} and replace its contents with the following:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/***************************************************************************//**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@file}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{app.c}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ TinyML Sine Wave Predictor application}
\CommentTok{ *******************************************************************************}
\CommentTok{ * \# License}
\CommentTok{ * }\KeywordTok{\textless{}b\textgreater{}}\CommentTok{Copyright 2023 Silicon Laboratories Inc. www.silabs.com}\KeywordTok{\textless{}/b\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{ *******************************************************************************}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{SPDX{-}License{-}Identifier:}\CommentTok{ Zlib}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * The licensor of this software is Silicon Laboratories Inc.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * This software is provided \textquotesingle{}as{-}is\textquotesingle{}, without any express or implied}
\CommentTok{ * warranty. In no event will the authors be held liable for any damages}
\CommentTok{ * arising from the use of this software.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * Permission is granted to anyone to use this software for any purpose,}
\CommentTok{ * including commercial applications, and to alter it and redistribute it}
\CommentTok{ * freely, subject to the following restrictions:}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * 1. The origin of this software must not be misrepresented; you must not}
\CommentTok{ *    claim that you wrote the original software. If you use this software}
\CommentTok{ *    in a product, an acknowledgment in the product documentation would be}
\CommentTok{ *    appreciated but is not required.}
\CommentTok{ * 2. Altered source versions must be plainly marked as such, and must not be}
\CommentTok{ *    misrepresented as being the original software.}
\CommentTok{ * 3. This notice may not be removed or altered from any source distribution.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ ******************************************************************************/}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_component\_catalog.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_system\_init.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"app.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#if defined(SL\_CATALOG\_POWER\_MANAGER\_PRESENT)}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_power\_manager.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_system\_process\_action.h"}

\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stdio.h\textgreater{}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}math.h\textgreater{}}

\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_tflite\_micro\_model.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_led.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_pwm.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_sleeptimer.h"}

\CommentTok{// Constants for sine wave demonstration}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE  }\DecValTok{32}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define X\_RANGE               }\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{2.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\PreprocessorTok{ }\OperatorTok{*}\PreprocessorTok{ }\FloatTok{3.14159265359}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{)}\PreprocessorTok{  }\CommentTok{// 2π radians}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define PWM\_FREQUENCY\_HZ      }\DecValTok{10000}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS }\DecValTok{50}

\CommentTok{// Global variables}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ inference\_count }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ app\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// Initialize TFLite model}
\NormalTok{  sl\_status\_t status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_init}\OperatorTok{();}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to initialize TensorFlow Lite Micro}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Initialize PWM for LED control}
\NormalTok{  sl\_pwm\_config\_t pwm\_config }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{frequency }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ PWM\_FREQUENCY\_HZ}\OperatorTok{,}
    \OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{polarity }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SL\_PWM\_ACTIVE\_HIGH}
  \OperatorTok{\};}
  
\NormalTok{  sl\_pwm\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_PWM\_LED0}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{pwm\_config}\OperatorTok{);}
  
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Sine Wave Predictor initialized}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ app\_process\_action}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// Calculate x value based on our position in the cycle}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ position }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{/} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE}\OperatorTok{;}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ position }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{// Prepare the input tensor with our x value}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ input\_data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\{}\NormalTok{ x\_val }\OperatorTok{\};}
\NormalTok{  sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  sl\_status\_t status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to get input tensor}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Copy our input data to the input tensor}
\NormalTok{  status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_set\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ input\_data}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{input\_data}\OperatorTok{));}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to set input tensor data}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Run inference}
\NormalTok{  status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_invoke}\OperatorTok{();}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Inference failed}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Get the output tensor}
\NormalTok{  sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to get output tensor}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Get the predicted sine value}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ predicted\_sine }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{predicted\_sine}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{predicted\_sine}\OperatorTok{));}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to get output tensor data}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Map the sine value ({-}1 to 1) to PWM duty cycle (0 to 100\%)}
  \DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\OperatorTok{)((}\NormalTok{predicted\_sine }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{1.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{50.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Set LED brightness using PWM}
\NormalTok{  sl\_pwm\_set\_duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_PWM\_LED0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Log the values (only every 8th inference to reduce console traffic)}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{8} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"x: }\SpecialCharTok{\%.3f}\StringTok{, predicted sine: }\SpecialCharTok{\%.3f}\StringTok{, duty cycle: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d\%\%\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} 
\NormalTok{           x\_val}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ predicted\_sine}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Increment the inference counter}
\NormalTok{  inference\_count}\OperatorTok{++;}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    inference\_count }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Add a delay before the next inference}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Creating the Model Integration
File}\label{creating-the-model-integration-file}

Create a new file called \texttt{sl\_tflite\_micro\_model.c} in the
\texttt{src} folder with the following content:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_tflite\_micro\_model.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sine\_model\_data.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stdio.h\textgreater{}}

\CommentTok{// TensorFlow Lite for Microcontrollers components}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/all\_ops\_resolver.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_error\_reporter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_interpreter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/schema/schema\_generated.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/version.h"}

\CommentTok{// Global variables}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroErrorReporter micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{ErrorReporter}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ error\_reporter }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{const}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{Model}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ model }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroInterpreter}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ interpreter }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ input\_tensor }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ output\_tensor }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Create an area of memory for input, output, and intermediate arrays}
\KeywordTok{constexpr} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ kTensorArenaSize }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{8} \OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{1024}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{kTensorArenaSize}\OperatorTok{];}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// Map the model into a usable data structure}
\NormalTok{  model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{GetModel}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{g\_sine\_model\_data}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{model}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{version}\OperatorTok{()} \OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ TFLITE\_SCHEMA\_VERSION}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Model version mismatch: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{ vs }\SpecialCharTok{\%d\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ model}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{version}\OperatorTok{(),}\NormalTok{ TFLITE\_SCHEMA\_VERSION}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_FAIL}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Create an all operations resolver}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{AllOpsResolver resolver}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{// Build an interpreter to run the model}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroInterpreter static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{    model}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ resolver}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ kTensorArenaSize}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  interpreter }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{// Allocate memory for all tensors}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{AllocateTensors}\OperatorTok{();}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"AllocateTensors() failed}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_ALLOCATION\_FAILED}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Get pointers to the model\textquotesingle{}s input and output tensors}
\NormalTok{  input\_tensor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{input}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  output\_tensor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{output}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Check that input and output tensors are the expected size and type}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{size }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||} 
\NormalTok{      input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteFloat32}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Unexpected input tensor format}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_INVALID\_PARAMETER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{size }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||} 
\NormalTok{      output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteFloat32}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Unexpected output tensor format}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_INVALID\_PARAMETER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"TensorFlow Lite Micro initialized successfully}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ index}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ index }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{inputs\_size}\OperatorTok{())} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_INVALID\_PARAMETER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
\NormalTok{  tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{input}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{index}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ index}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ index }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{outputs\_size}\OperatorTok{())} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_INVALID\_PARAMETER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
\NormalTok{  tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{output}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{index}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_set\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                            \DataTypeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                            \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ data }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_NULL\_POINTER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Size check based on tensor type and dims}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
  \ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{size}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{];}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteFloat32}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteInt8}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int8\_t}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteUInt8}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_NOT\_SUPPORTED}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{size }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ tensor\_size}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_WOULD\_OVERFLOW}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Copy the data to the tensor}
\NormalTok{  memcpy}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{raw}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                            \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                            \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ data }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_NULL\_POINTER}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Size check based on tensor type and dims}
  \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
  \ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{size}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{];}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteFloat32}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteInt8}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int8\_t}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteUInt8}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    tensor\_size }\OperatorTok{*=} \KeywordTok{sizeof}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_NOT\_SUPPORTED}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{size }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ tensor\_size}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_WOULD\_OVERFLOW}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Copy the data from the tensor}
\NormalTok{  memcpy}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{tensor}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{raw}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_invoke}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter }\OperatorTok{==} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_NOT\_INITIALIZED}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{Invoke}\OperatorTok{();}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_FAIL}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now, create the header file \texttt{sl\_tflite\_micro\_model.h} in the
\texttt{inc} folder:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#ifndef SL\_TFLITE\_MICRO\_MODEL\_H}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define SL\_TFLITE\_MICRO\_MODEL\_H}

\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_status.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stdint.h\textgreater{}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stddef.h\textgreater{}}

\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\KeywordTok{extern} \StringTok{"C"} \OperatorTok{\{}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}

\CommentTok{// Forward declarations from TensorFlow Lite}
\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\NormalTok{namespace tflite }\OperatorTok{\{}
\KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}  \CommentTok{// namespace tflite}
\KeywordTok{typedef} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{TfLiteTensor TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\PreprocessorTok{\#else}
\KeywordTok{typedef} \KeywordTok{struct}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}

\CommentTok{// Tensor structure}
\KeywordTok{typedef} \KeywordTok{struct} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Initialize TensorFlow Lite Micro with the sine model}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Get an input tensor by index}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{index}\CommentTok{ Index of the input tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{tensor}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the tensor structure to fill}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ index}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Get an output tensor by index}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{index}\CommentTok{ Index of the output tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{tensor}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the tensor structure to fill}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_output\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ index}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Set data to a tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{tensor}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{data}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the data to copy}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{size}\CommentTok{ Size of the data in bytes}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_set\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                          \DataTypeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                          \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Get data from a tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{tensor}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the tensor}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{data}\CommentTok{ Pointer to the buffer to receive the data}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{size}\CommentTok{ Size of the buffer in bytes}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_tensor\_data}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tensor}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                          \DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{,} 
                                          \DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ size}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Run inference using the TensorFlow Lite model}
\CommentTok{ * }
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK if successful}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_micro\_invoke}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{);}

\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}

\PreprocessorTok{\#endif }\CommentTok{// SL\_TFLITE\_MICRO\_MODEL\_H}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Building and Flashing the
Application}\label{building-and-flashing-the-application}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  In Simplicity Studio, right-click on the project and select ``Build
  Project''
\item
  After successful compilation, right-click again and select ``Run As''
  → ``Silicon Labs ARM Program''
\item
  The application will be flashed to your EFR32MG24 device and start
  running
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Observing the Results}\label{observing-the-results}

Once your application is running on the EFR32MG24 device:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The LED will pulse with brightness that follows the sine wave pattern
\item
  Open the Serial Console in Simplicity Studio to view debug output
\item
  You'll see logs showing the input value, predicted sine value, and the
  corresponding LED duty cycle
\end{enumerate}

\section{How it Works: Understanding the
Implementation}\label{how-it-works-understanding-the-implementation}

Our TinyML sine wave predictor consists of several key components:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Model Training and Conversion}: Using Google Colab, we trained
  a small neural network to approximate the sine function and converted
  it to TF Lite format, then to a C array.
\item
  \textbf{TensorFlow Lite Micro Integration}: We've implemented a simple
  wrapper around TF Lite Micro's C++ API, providing a clean C interface
  for our application.
\item
  \textbf{Application Logic}: The main application loop:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Calculates an x value based on where we are in the cycle
  \item
    Feeds this value into the model
  \item
    Retrieves the predicted sine value
  \item
    Maps the prediction to LED brightness via PWM
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Visual Output}: The LED brightness follows a sine wave
  pattern, providing visual confirmation that our model is working
  correctly.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Extending the TinyML
Application}\label{extending-the-tinyml-application}

Now that we have our basic ``Hello World'' TinyML application running,
there are several ways we can extend and enhance it:

\subsection{1. Adding Multiple LED
Support}\label{adding-multiple-led-support}

For devices with multiple LEDs, we can create more interesting visual
patterns by controlling multiple LEDs based on different phases of the
sine wave:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// In app.c, add phase offsets for each LED}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define LED\_COUNT }\DecValTok{4}\PreprocessorTok{  }\CommentTok{// Assuming 4 available LEDs}
\DataTypeTok{const} \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ phase\_offsets}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{LED\_COUNT}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\{}
  \FloatTok{0.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{,}                  \CommentTok{// LED0: No phase offset}
  \FloatTok{0.5}\BuiltInTok{f} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{4.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{,} \CommentTok{// LED1: 45 degrees offset}
\NormalTok{  X\_RANGE }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{4.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{,}        \CommentTok{// LED2: 90 degrees offset}
  \FloatTok{1.5}\BuiltInTok{f} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{4.0}\BuiltInTok{f}  \CommentTok{// LED3: 135 degrees offset}
\OperatorTok{\};}

\CommentTok{// Then in app\_process\_action(), add a loop to control all LEDs}
\ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ LED\_COUNT}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// Calculate offset x value for this LED}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ led\_x\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_val }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ phase\_offsets}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{];}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{led\_x\_val }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    led\_x\_val }\OperatorTok{{-}=}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE}\OperatorTok{;}  \CommentTok{// Wrap around to stay in range}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{// Prepare input tensor with our x value}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ input\_data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\{}\NormalTok{ led\_x\_val }\OperatorTok{\};}
\NormalTok{  sl\_tflite\_micro\_tensor\_t input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_get\_input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{input\_tensor}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \ControlFlowTok{continue}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{// Set tensor data, invoke model, and get output as before...}
  
  \CommentTok{// Set corresponding LED brightness}
  \DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\OperatorTok{)((}\NormalTok{predicted\_sine }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{1.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{50.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  sl\_pwm\_set\_duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{);}  \CommentTok{// Assuming LED PWM instances are indexed}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{2. Adding LCD Display
Support}\label{adding-lcd-display-support}

If your EFR32MG24 development board includes an LCD display, you can
visualize the sine wave more directly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// In app.c, add LCD{-}related includes}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_glib.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_lcd.h"}

\CommentTok{// Add LCD dimensions and buffers}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define LCD\_WIDTH  }\DecValTok{128}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define LCD\_HEIGHT }\DecValTok{64}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define HISTORY\_LENGTH }\NormalTok{LCD\_WIDTH}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{];}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ history\_index }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Initialize the LCD in app\_init()}
\NormalTok{sl\_simple\_lcd\_init}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{sl\_glib\_init}\OperatorTok{();}
\CommentTok{// Clear history buffer}
\ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{// In app\_process\_action(), after getting the predicted sine:}
\CommentTok{// Store in history buffer}
\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{history\_index}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ predicted\_sine}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Every few inferences, update the display}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_Context\_t context}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  sl\_glib\_get\_context}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Clear display}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_clear}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Draw x and y axes}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_drawLineH}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ LCD\_WIDTH}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_drawLineV}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Draw the sine wave}
  \ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ x1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{;}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ y1 }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{{-}} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{));}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ x2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ y2 }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{{-}} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_LENGTH}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ LCD\_HEIGHT}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{));}
\NormalTok{    GLIB\_drawLine}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ x1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ y1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ x2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ y2}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
