  \CommentTok{// Update display}
\NormalTok{  sl\_glib\_update\_display}\OperatorTok{();}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{3. Implementing Power
Optimization}\label{implementing-power-optimization}

To make our TinyML application more power-efficient for battery-powered
operation, we can add sleep modes between inferences:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Replace the fixed delay with sleep mode}
\CommentTok{// Instead of: sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond(INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS);}

\PreprocessorTok{\#if defined(SL\_CATALOG\_POWER\_MANAGER\_PRESENT)}
  \CommentTok{// Schedule next wakeup}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_tick\_t ticks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_sleeptimer\_ms\_to\_tick}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  sl\_power\_manager\_schedule\_wakeup}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{ticks}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{// Enter sleep mode}
\NormalTok{  sl\_power\_manager\_sleep}\OperatorTok{();}
\PreprocessorTok{\#else}
  \CommentTok{// Fall back to delay if power manager isn\textquotesingle{}t available}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{4. Enhanced User Interface with
Buttons}\label{enhanced-user-interface-with-buttons}

We can use the buttons on the development board to control aspects of
the application:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Include button support}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_button.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_button.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_button\_btn0\_config.h"}

\CommentTok{// Add state variables}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ paused }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{false}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ speed\_factor }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{// In app\_init, initialize buttons}
\NormalTok{sl\_button\_init}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{sl\_button\_btn0}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Check button state in app\_process\_action}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_button\_get\_state}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{sl\_button\_btn0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ SL\_SIMPLE\_BUTTON\_PRESSED}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{// Toggle pause state}
\NormalTok{  paused }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{paused}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Application }\SpecialCharTok{\%s\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ paused }\OperatorTok{?} \StringTok{"paused"} \OperatorTok{:} \StringTok{"resumed"}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{// Only update inference\_count if not paused}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(!}\NormalTok{paused}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  inference\_count }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ inference\_count }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{5. Performance Profiling and
Optimization}\label{performance-profiling-and-optimization}

To understand and optimize the performance of our TinyML application, we
can add timing measurements:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Add profiling includes}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"em\_cmu.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"em\_timer.h"}

\CommentTok{// Setup timer for profiling in app\_init()}
\NormalTok{CMU\_ClockEnable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{cmuClock\_TIMER1}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{true}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Init\_TypeDef timerInit }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TIMER\_INIT\_DEFAULT}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER1}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{timerInit}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// In app\_process\_action(), measure inference time}
\CommentTok{// Reset timer}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_CounterSet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER1}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Start timer}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Enable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER1}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{true}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Run inference}
\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_micro\_invoke}\OperatorTok{();}

\CommentTok{// Stop timer and read value}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Enable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER1}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{false}\OperatorTok{);}
\DataTypeTok{uint32\_t}\NormalTok{ ticks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TIMER\_CounterGet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER1}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Convert ticks to microseconds (depends on timer clock)}
\DataTypeTok{uint32\_t}\NormalTok{ us }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ticks }\OperatorTok{/} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{CMU\_ClockFreqGet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{cmuClock\_TIMER1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{1000000}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Log every Nth inference}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{10} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Inference time: }\SpecialCharTok{\%lu}\StringTok{ microseconds}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ us}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Building TinyML Applications with the Gecko
SDK}\label{building-tinyml-applications-with-the-gecko-sdk}

Compared to the traditional TinyML approach with direct TensorFlow Lite
for Microcontrollers integration, the Gecko SDK approach offers several
advantages for EFR32 developers:

\subsection{Simplified Project Setup}\label{simplified-project-setup}

The Gecko SDK provides a structured approach to project creation with
built-in TinyML support:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Project Templates}: Simplicity Studio's project wizard
  includes TinyML templates that set up the necessary directory
  structure, build configuration, and initial code.
\item
  \textbf{Integrated Build System}: The SDK handles compiler flags,
  library dependencies, and linking, eliminating the need for custom
  Makefiles.
\item
  \textbf{Hardware Abstraction Layer (HAL)}: The SDK provides
  hardware-specific drivers and APIs for peripherals like PWM, GPIOs,
  and timers, making it easier to integrate TinyML with device hardware.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Streamlined Development
Workflow}\label{streamlined-development-workflow}

The development workflow with Simplicity Studio and Gecko SDK is
straightforward:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Model Training}: Use Google Colab or TensorFlow on your
  computer to train and convert models.
\item
  \textbf{Project Creation}: Launch Simplicity Studio, select the
  TensorFlow Lite Micro example template, and create a new project.
\item
  \textbf{Model Integration}: Import your model header file into the
  project.
\item
  \textbf{Application Logic}: Write code in C to initialize the model,
  prepare inputs, run inference, and process outputs.
\item
  \textbf{Build and Flash}: Use Simplicity Studio's integrated tools to
  compile the code and flash it to your device.
\item
  \textbf{Debug and Monitor}: The Serial Console and Energy Profiler
  tools help monitor application behavior and optimize performance.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Hardware-Specific
Optimizations}\label{hardware-specific-optimizations}

The Gecko SDK includes optimizations specifically for the EFR32
platform:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Memory Optimization}: Memory management is tuned for the
  EFR32's memory architecture.
\item
  \textbf{Power Management}: Integration with the Energy Management Unit
  (EMU) allows for fine-grained control over active, sleep, and deep
  sleep states.
\item
  \textbf{Peripheral Control}: Direct access to hardware accelerators
  and peripherals that can enhance TinyML performance.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Conclusion}\label{conclusion-1}

The sine wave predictor represents an elegant ``Hello World'' example of
TinyML deployment on the EFR32MG24 platform. While seemingly simple,
this implementation encompasses all the key elements of machine learning
on microcontrollers:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Model design with consideration for resource constraints
\item
  Training and evaluation on standard datasets
\item
  Quantization and optimization for embedded deployment
\item
  Integration with Simplicity Studio and Gecko SDK
\item
  Hardware output integration via GPIO and PWM capabilities
\end{enumerate}

Through this foundation, developers can extend to more complex TinyML
applications on the EFR32MG24, such as sensor fusion, predictive
maintenance, anomaly detection, and keyword spotting---all within a
power envelope suitable for long-term battery-powered operation.

The techniques demonstrated here---model quantization, C code
generation, and deployment with Simplicity Studio---provide a template
that can be adapted for more sophisticated machine learning tasks,
enabling a new class of intelligent edge devices based on the EFR32MG24
platform.

\chapter{Building a TinyML
Application}\label{building-a-tinyml-application}

In the previous chapter, we trained a neural network model to predict
sine wave values and prepared it for deployment on an EFR32MG24
microcontroller. Now we'll build a complete application around this
model and deploy it to the hardware. This chapter focuses on the
practical aspects of implementing TinyML using Silicon Labs' Gecko SDK
and Simplicity Studio rather than the traditional TensorFlow Lite for
Microcontrollers approach.

\section{Understanding the Gecko SDK Approach to
TinyML}\label{understanding-the-gecko-sdk-approach-to-tinyml}

The Gecko SDK provides a structured approach to embedded development
specifically optimized for Silicon Labs' microcontrollers. This offers
several advantages over the more generic TinyML implementations:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pre-integrated Components}: The SDK includes optimized
  TensorFlow Lite Micro components already configured for EFR32 devices
\item
  \textbf{Hardware Abstraction Layer}: Direct integration with EFR32
  peripherals through a consistent API
\item
  \textbf{Project Templates}: Simplicity Studio provides starting points
  for TinyML applications
\item
  \textbf{Advanced Tooling}: Debugging, energy profiling, and
  configuration tools are built into the development environment
\end{enumerate}

\section{Setting Up Your Development
Environment}\label{setting-up-your-development-environment}

Before we begin building our application, ensure you have the following
tools installed:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Simplicity Studio 5}: Download and install from
  \href{https://www.silabs.com/developers/simplicity-studio}{Silicon
  Labs' website}
\item
  \textbf{Gecko SDK}: The latest version will be installed through
  Simplicity Studio
\item
  \textbf{J-Link Drivers}: These should be installed with Simplicity
  Studio
\item
  \textbf{EFR32MG24 Development Board}: Connect this to your computer
  via USB
\end{enumerate}

\section{Creating a New Project in Simplicity
Studio}\label{creating-a-new-project-in-simplicity-studio-1}

Let's start by creating a TinyML project in Simplicity Studio:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Launch Simplicity Studio 5
\item
  In the Launcher perspective, click on your connected EFR32MG24 device
\item
  Click ``Create New Project'' in the ``Overview'' tab
\item
  Select ``Silicon Labs Project Wizard'' and click ``NEXT''
\item
  In the SDK Selection dialog, ensure the latest Gecko SDK is selected
  and click ``NEXT''
\item
  In the Project Generation dialog:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Filter for ``example'' in the search box
  \item
    Select ``TensorFlow Lite Micro Example''
  \item
    Click ``NEXT''
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Configure your project:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Name: \texttt{sine\_wave\_predictor}
  \item
    Keep the default location
  \item
    Click ``FINISH''
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

Simplicity Studio will generate a project with the necessary components
for a TinyML application. Let's explore the project structure before
making our modifications.

\section{Exploring the Project
Structure}\label{exploring-the-project-structure}

The generated project includes several important directories and files:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{config/}: Contains hardware configuration files for your
  specific board
\item
  \textbf{gecko\_sdk/}: The Gecko SDK source code, including TensorFlow
  Lite Micro
\item
  \textbf{autogen/}: Auto-generated initialization code for the device
\item
  \textbf{app.c}: Your application's main source file
\item
  \textbf{app.h}: Header file for your application
\end{itemize}

The TensorFlow Lite Micro example comes with a sample model that
classifies motion patterns. We'll replace this with our sine wave model.

\section{Importing the Sine Wave
Model}\label{importing-the-sine-wave-model}

First, let's import the sine model data that we generated in the
previous chapter:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Right-click on the project in the ``Project Explorer'' view
\item
  Select ``Import'' → ``General'' → ``File System''
\item
  Browse to the location where you saved \texttt{sine\_model\_data.h}
\item
  Select the file and click ``Finish''
\end{enumerate}

The model data will be added to your project. Now let's modify the
application code to use our sine wave model.

\section{Implementing the
Application}\label{implementing-the-application}

Let's replace the content of \texttt{app.c} with our sine wave
prediction application code. Open \texttt{app.c} and replace its
contents with the following:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/***************************************************************************//**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@file}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{app.c}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ TinyML Sine Wave Predictor application}
\CommentTok{ *******************************************************************************}
\CommentTok{ * \# License}
\CommentTok{ * }\KeywordTok{\textless{}b\textgreater{}}\CommentTok{Copyright 2023 Silicon Laboratories Inc. www.silabs.com}\KeywordTok{\textless{}/b\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{ *******************************************************************************}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{SPDX{-}License{-}Identifier:}\CommentTok{ Zlib}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * The licensor of this software is Silicon Laboratories Inc.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * This software is provided \textquotesingle{}as{-}is\textquotesingle{}, without any express or implied}
\CommentTok{ * warranty. In no event will the authors be held liable for any damages}
\CommentTok{ * arising from the use of this software.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * Permission is granted to anyone to use this software for any purpose,}
\CommentTok{ * including commercial applications, and to alter it and redistribute it}
\CommentTok{ * freely, subject to the following restrictions:}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * 1. The origin of this software must not be misrepresented; you must not}
\CommentTok{ *    claim that you wrote the original software. If you use this software}
\CommentTok{ *    in a product, an acknowledgment in the product documentation would be}
\CommentTok{ *    appreciated but is not required.}
\CommentTok{ * 2. Altered source versions must be plainly marked as such, and must not be}
\CommentTok{ *    misrepresented as being the original software.}
\CommentTok{ * 3. This notice may not be removed or altered from any source distribution.}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ ******************************************************************************/}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_component\_catalog.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_system\_init.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"app.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#if defined(SL\_CATALOG\_POWER\_MANAGER\_PRESENT)}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_power\_manager.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_system\_process\_action.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Additional includes for our application */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stdio.h\textgreater{}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}math.h\textgreater{}}

\CommentTok{/* Include TensorFlow Lite components */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/all\_ops\_resolver.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_error\_reporter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_interpreter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/schema/schema\_generated.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"tensorflow/lite/version.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Include our model data */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sine\_model\_data.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Include hardware control components */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_led.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_led\_instances.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_sleeptimer.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Constants for sine wave demonstration */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE  }\DecValTok{32}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define X\_RANGE               }\OperatorTok{(}\FloatTok{2.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\PreprocessorTok{ }\OperatorTok{*}\PreprocessorTok{ }\FloatTok{3.14159265359}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{)}\PreprocessorTok{  }\CommentTok{/* 2π radians */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS }\DecValTok{50}

\CommentTok{/* Global variables for TensorFlow Lite model */}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroErrorReporter micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{ErrorReporter}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ error\_reporter }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{;}
\CommentTok{/* We\textquotesingle{}ll use the C version of TensorFlow Lite Micro API */}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroInterpreter}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ interpreter }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ input }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ output }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{nullptr}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/* Create an area of memory for input, output, and intermediate arrays */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE }\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\PreprocessorTok{ }\OperatorTok{*}\PreprocessorTok{ }\DecValTok{1024}\OperatorTok{)}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{];}

\CommentTok{/* Application state variables */}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ inference\_count }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/***************************************************************************//**}
\CommentTok{ * Initialize application.}
\CommentTok{ ******************************************************************************/}
\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ app\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{/* Map the model into a usable data structure */}
\NormalTok{  model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{GetModel}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{g\_sine\_model\_data}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{model}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{version}\OperatorTok{()} \OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ TFLITE\_SCHEMA\_VERSION}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{Report}\OperatorTok{(}
        \StringTok{"Model provided is schema version }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{ not equal "}
        \StringTok{"to supported version }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{.}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}
\NormalTok{        model}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{version}\OperatorTok{(),}\NormalTok{ TFLITE\_SCHEMA\_VERSION}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* This pulls in all the operation implementations we need */}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{AllOpsResolver resolver}\OperatorTok{;}

  \CommentTok{/* Build an interpreter to run the model with */}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroInterpreter static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{(}
\NormalTok{      model}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ resolver}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  interpreter }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{;}

  \CommentTok{/* Allocate memory from the tensor\_arena for the model\textquotesingle{}s tensors */}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{AllocateTensors}\OperatorTok{();}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{Report}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"AllocateTensors() failed"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Obtain pointers to the model\textquotesingle{}s input and output tensors */}
\NormalTok{  input }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{input}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{output}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}

  \CommentTok{/* Check that input tensor dimensions are as expected */}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{size }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||} 
\NormalTok{      input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteFloat32}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{Report}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Unexpected input tensor dimensions or type"}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Initialize LED */}
\NormalTok{  sl\_led\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_SIMPLE\_LED\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{));}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Print initialization message */}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Sine Wave Predictor initialized successfully!}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Model input dims: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{, type: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} 
\NormalTok{         input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{],}\NormalTok{ input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{dims}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{],}\NormalTok{ input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{type}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{/***************************************************************************//**}
\CommentTok{ * App ticking function.}
\CommentTok{ ******************************************************************************/}
\DataTypeTok{void}\NormalTok{ app\_process\_action}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{/* Calculate an x value to feed into the model based on current inference count */}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ position }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{inference\_count}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{/} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE}\OperatorTok{);}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ position }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Set the input tensor with our calculated x value */}
\NormalTok{  input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_val}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Run inference, and report any error */}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus invoke\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter\_Invoke}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{invoke\_status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Invoke failed on x\_val: }\SpecialCharTok{\%f\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{double}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{x\_val}\OperatorTok{);}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Read the predicted y value from the model\textquotesingle{}s output tensor */}
  \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ y\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ output}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{];}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Map the sine output ({-}1 to 1) to LED brightness }
\CommentTok{   * For simplicity, we\textquotesingle{}ll just turn the LED on when the value is positive}
\CommentTok{   * and off when it\textquotesingle{}s negative. For PWM control, you would need to }
\CommentTok{   * configure a PWM peripheral. */}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{y\_val }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    sl\_led\_turn\_on}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_SIMPLE\_LED\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{));}
  \OperatorTok{\}} \ControlFlowTok{else} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    sl\_led\_turn\_off}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_SIMPLE\_LED\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{));}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Log every 4th inference to avoid flooding the console */}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"x\_value: }\SpecialCharTok{\%f}\StringTok{, predicted\_sine: }\SpecialCharTok{\%f\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{double}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{x\_val}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{double}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{y\_val}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Increment the inference\_count, and reset it if we have reached}
\CommentTok{   * the total number per cycle */}
\NormalTok{  inference\_count }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ INFERENCES\_PER\_CYCLE}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{ inference\_count }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Add a delay between inferences */}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This application will: 1. Initialize the TensorFlow Lite Micro
interpreter with our sine model 2. Set up an LED for output 3. In each
loop iteration: - Calculate an x value within our 0 to 2π range - Run
inference to get the predicted sine value - Toggle the LED based on
whether the sine value is positive or negative - Log the values to the
console - Increment the inference counter

\section{Enhancing Output with PWM
Control}\label{enhancing-output-with-pwm-control}

The basic application just toggles an LED, but we can create a more
interesting visualization by controlling LED brightness with PWM. Let's
create a PWM component for our project:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Right-click on your project in the Project Explorer
\item
  Select ``Configure Project''
\item
  Click on ``SOFTWARE COMPONENTS''
\item
  In the search box, type ``PWM''
\item
  Find ``PWM Driver'' → ``Simple PWM'' and click ``Install''
\item
  Click ``Force Install'' if prompted
\item
  Click ``DONE'' to save the configuration
\end{enumerate}

Now, modify the application code to use PWM for LED brightness control.
Replace the LED control section in \texttt{app\_process\_action()} with:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Map the sine output ({-}1 to 1) to PWM duty cycle (0 to 100\%) */}
\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\OperatorTok{)((}\NormalTok{y\_val }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{1.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{50.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{);}
  
\CommentTok{/* Set LED brightness using PWM */}
\NormalTok{sl\_pwm\_set\_duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_PWM\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{),}\NormalTok{ duty\_cycle}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Also, add the PWM initialization in the \texttt{app\_init()} function
after the LED initialization:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Initialize PWM for LED brightness control */}
\NormalTok{sl\_pwm\_config\_t pwm\_config }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\{}
  \OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{frequency }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{10000}\OperatorTok{,}  \CommentTok{/* 10 kHz PWM frequency */}
  \OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{polarity }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SL\_PWM\_ACTIVE\_HIGH}
\OperatorTok{\};}
\NormalTok{sl\_pwm\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_PWM\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{),} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{pwm\_config}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Don't forget to include the PWM header at the top of the file:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_pwm.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_pwm\_instances.h"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Building and Flashing the
Application}\label{building-and-flashing-the-application-1}

Now let's build and flash our application to the EFR32MG24 board:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Right-click on your project in the Project Explorer
\item
  Select ``Build Project''
\item
  Once the build completes successfully, right-click again on the
  project
\item
  Select ``Run As'' → ``Silicon Labs ARM Program''
\end{enumerate}

Simplicity Studio will compile your code, flash it to the device, and
start execution.

\section{Debugging and Monitoring}\label{debugging-and-monitoring}

To monitor the output of your application:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  In Simplicity Studio, go to the ``Debug Adapters'' view
\item
  Right-click on your connected device and select ``Launch Console''
\item
  In the console dialog, select ``Serial 1'' and click ``OK''
\end{enumerate}

You should now see the application's output messages showing x values
and predicted sine values.

\section{Optimizing TinyML
Performance}\label{optimizing-tinyml-performance}

\subsection{Memory Optimization}\label{memory-optimization}

TinyML applications on microcontrollers must be memory-efficient. Let's
look at ways to optimize memory usage:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Tensor Arena Size}: Reduce the \texttt{TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE} to
  the minimum required. Try starting with 10KB and reducing it
  incrementally:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#define TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE }\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\PreprocessorTok{ }\OperatorTok{*}\PreprocessorTok{ }\DecValTok{1024}\OperatorTok{)}\PreprocessorTok{  }\CommentTok{/* Start with 10KB */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can determine the minimum required size by adding debug output:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Add this after interpreter{-}\textgreater{}AllocateTensors() in app\_init() */}
\DataTypeTok{size\_t}\NormalTok{ used\_bytes }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{arena\_used\_bytes}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Model uses }\SpecialCharTok{\%d}\StringTok{ bytes of tensor arena}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{used\_bytes}\OperatorTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Selective Op Resolution}: Instead of using
  \texttt{AllOpsResolver}, create a custom resolver with only the
  operations needed:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Replace AllOpsResolver with this */}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ tflite}\OperatorTok{::}\NormalTok{MicroMutableOpResolver}\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ resolver}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{resolver}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{AddFullyConnected}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{resolver}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{AddRelu}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{resolver}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{AddAdd}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{resolver}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{AddMul}\OperatorTok{();}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Power Optimization}\label{power-optimization}

For battery-powered applications, power efficiency is critical:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Sleep Between Inferences}: Replace the simple delay with a
  power-efficient sleep:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Replace sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond() with: */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#if defined(SL\_CATALOG\_POWER\_MANAGER\_PRESENT)}
  \CommentTok{/* Schedule next wakeup */}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_tick\_t ticks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_sleeptimer\_ms\_to\_tick}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  sl\_power\_manager\_schedule\_wakeup}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{ticks}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Enter sleep mode */}
\NormalTok{  sl\_power\_manager\_sleep}\OperatorTok{();}
\PreprocessorTok{\#else}
  \CommentTok{/* Fall back to delay if power manager isn\textquotesingle{}t available */}
\NormalTok{  sl\_sleeptimer\_delay\_millisecond}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{INFERENCE\_INTERVAL\_MS}\OperatorTok{);}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Measurement with Energy Profiler}: Simplicity Studio includes
  an Energy Profiler tool to measure power consumption:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Connect your board with the Advanced Energy Monitor (AEM)
  \item
    In Simplicity Studio, go to Tools → Energy Profiler
  \item
    Start a capture while your application is running
  \item
    Analyze current consumption during inference and sleep periods
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Timing Performance}\label{timing-performance}

To measure inference time:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Add these includes */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"em\_cmu.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"em\_timer.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Initialize timer in app\_init() */}
\NormalTok{CMU\_ClockEnable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{cmuClock\_TIMER0}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{true}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Init\_TypeDef timerInit }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TIMER\_INIT\_DEFAULT}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER0}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{timerInit}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_process\_action(), surround the inference with timing code */}
\CommentTok{/* Reset and start timer */}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_CounterSet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER0}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Enable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER0}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{true}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/* Run inference */}
\NormalTok{TfLiteStatus invoke\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ interpreter}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{Invoke}\OperatorTok{();}

\CommentTok{/* Stop timer and read counter */}
\NormalTok{TIMER\_Enable}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER0}\OperatorTok{,} \KeywordTok{false}\OperatorTok{);}
\DataTypeTok{uint32\_t}\NormalTok{ ticks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TIMER\_CounterGet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{TIMER0}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/* Convert ticks to microseconds */}
\DataTypeTok{uint32\_t}\NormalTok{ us }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ticks }\OperatorTok{/} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{CMU\_ClockFreqGet}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{cmuClock\_TIMER0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{1000000}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/* Log timing information */}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{10} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Inference took }\SpecialCharTok{\%lu}\StringTok{ microseconds}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ us}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Adding User Interaction with
Buttons}\label{adding-user-interaction-with-buttons}

We can make our application more interactive by using buttons to control
its behavior:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Add a button component to your project:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Right-click on your project and select ``Configure Project''
  \item
    Go to ``SOFTWARE COMPONENTS''
  \item
    Search for ``button'' and install ``Simple Button'' components
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Modify your code to handle button presses:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Include button headers */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_button.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_button\_instances.h"}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_init() */}
\CommentTok{/* Initialize buttons */}
\NormalTok{sl\_button\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_SIMPLE\_BUTTON\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{));}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_process\_action(), check for button press */}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{sl\_button\_get\_state}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{SL\_SIMPLE\_BUTTON\_INSTANCE}\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{))} \OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ SL\_SIMPLE\_BUTTON\_PRESSED}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{/* Toggle between normal speed and double speed */}
  \DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{bool}\NormalTok{ fast\_mode }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{false}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  fast\_mode }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{fast\_mode}\OperatorTok{;}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Update the inference interval */}
\NormalTok{  inference\_interval\_ms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ fast\_mode }\OperatorTok{?} \DecValTok{25} \OperatorTok{:} \DecValTok{50}\OperatorTok{;}
  
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Speed set to }\SpecialCharTok{\%s\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ fast\_mode }\OperatorTok{?} \StringTok{"FAST"} \OperatorTok{:} \StringTok{"NORMAL"}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Enhanced Visualization with LCD (if
available)}\label{enhanced-visualization-with-lcd-if-available}

If your development board has an LCD display, you can create more
sophisticated visualizations:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Add the LCD components to your project:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    In the ``Configure Project'' dialog, search for ``lcd''
  \item
    Install the ``GLIB Graphics Library'' and ``Simple LCD''
  \end{itemize}
\item
  Modify your code to display the sine wave on the LCD:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{/* Include LCD headers */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_glib.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_simple\_lcd.h"}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_init() */}
\CommentTok{/* Initialize LCD */}
\NormalTok{sl\_simple\_lcd\_init}\OperatorTok{();}
\NormalTok{sl\_glib\_initialize}\OperatorTok{();}

\CommentTok{/* Define a buffer to store recent sine wave values */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define HISTORY\_SIZE }\DecValTok{128}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{HISTORY\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{];}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ history\_index }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/* Initialize history buffer */}
\ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_process\_action(), after getting the prediction */}
\CommentTok{/* Store the prediction in the history buffer */}
\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{history\_index}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ y\_val}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/* Every 8th inference, update the LCD */}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{inference\_count }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{8} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_Context\_t context}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  sl\_glib\_get\_context}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Clear the display */}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_clear}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Draw axes */}
  \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ mid\_y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ context}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{height }\OperatorTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_drawLineH}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ context}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{width }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ mid\_y}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Draw the sine wave */}
  \ControlFlowTok{for} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_SIZE }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{++)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ x1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{;}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ y1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mid\_y }\OperatorTok{{-}} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mid\_y }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{0.8}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{);}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ x2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{;}
    \DataTypeTok{int}\NormalTok{ y2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mid\_y }\OperatorTok{{-}} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)(}\NormalTok{sine\_history}\OperatorTok{[(}\NormalTok{history\_index }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ HISTORY\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mid\_y }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{0.8}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{);}
    
\NormalTok{    GLIB\_drawLine}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ x1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ y1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ x2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ y2}\OperatorTok{);}
  \OperatorTok{\}}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Update the display */}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_drawString}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{,} \StringTok{"Sine Wave Predictor"}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ GLIB\_ALIGN\_CENTER}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  GLIB\_update}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{context}\OperatorTok{);}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Creating a Custom Component for
TinyML}\label{creating-a-custom-component-for-tinyml}

To make your TinyML code more reusable, consider creating a custom Gecko
SDK component. Here's a simple approach:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Create a header file \texttt{sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor.h}:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#ifndef SL\_TFLITE\_SINE\_PREDICTOR\_H}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define SL\_TFLITE\_SINE\_PREDICTOR\_H}

\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_status.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}stdint.h\textgreater{}}

\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\KeywordTok{extern} \StringTok{"C"} \OperatorTok{\{}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Initialize the TinyML sine predictor}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK on success}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{);}

\CommentTok{/**}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@brief}\CommentTok{ Run inference with a given x value}
\CommentTok{ *}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{x\_val}\CommentTok{ Input value in range [0, 2π]}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@param}\CommentTok{ }\CommentVarTok{y\_val}\CommentTok{ Pointer to store the predicted sine value}
\CommentTok{ * }\AnnotationTok{@return}\CommentTok{ sl\_status\_t SL\_STATUS\_OK on success}
\CommentTok{ */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_predict}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x\_val}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ y\_val}\OperatorTok{);}

\PreprocessorTok{\#ifdef \_\_cplusplus}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\PreprocessorTok{\#endif}

\PreprocessorTok{\#endif }\CommentTok{/* SL\_TFLITE\_SINE\_PREDICTOR\_H */}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  Create an implementation file \texttt{sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor.c}:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sine\_model\_data.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{\textless{}string.h\textgreater{}}

\CommentTok{/* TensorFlow Lite components */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/micro/kernels/micro\_ops.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_error\_reporter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_interpreter.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/micro/micro\_mutable\_op\_resolver.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/schema/schema\_generated.h"}
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"third\_party/tflite{-}micro/tensorflow/lite/version.h"}

\CommentTok{/* Static variables for TensorFlow Lite model {-} C compatible structure */}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroErrorReporter micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ interpreter }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ input }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteTensor}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/* Create an area of memory for input, output, and intermediate arrays */}
\PreprocessorTok{\#define TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE }\OperatorTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\PreprocessorTok{ }\OperatorTok{*}\PreprocessorTok{ }\DecValTok{1024}\OperatorTok{)}
\DataTypeTok{static} \DataTypeTok{uint8\_t}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{];}

\CommentTok{/* C implementation for initialization */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_init}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{void}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \CommentTok{/* Map the model into a usable data structure */}
  \DataTypeTok{const}\NormalTok{ TfLiteModel}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TfLiteModelCreate}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{g\_sine\_model\_data}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ g\_sine\_model\_data\_len}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_FAIL}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Initialize error reporter */}
\NormalTok{  TF\_MicroErrorReporter\_Init}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{);}

  \CommentTok{/* Create an operation resolver with the operations we need */}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver\_Init}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \CommentTok{/* Add the operations needed for our model */}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver\_AddFullyConnected}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver\_AddRelu}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver\_AddMul}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteMicroMutableOpResolver\_AddAdd}\OperatorTok{(\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{);}

  \CommentTok{/* Build an interpreter to run the model */}
  \DataTypeTok{static}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{;}
\NormalTok{  TF\_MicroInterpreter\_Init}\OperatorTok{(}
      \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ model}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{op\_resolver}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{ tensor\_arena}\OperatorTok{,} 
\NormalTok{      TENSOR\_ARENA\_SIZE}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{micro\_error\_reporter}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  interpreter }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{static\_interpreter}\OperatorTok{;}

  \CommentTok{/* Allocate memory from the tensor\_arena for the model\textquotesingle{}s tensors */}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter\_AllocateTensors}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{allocate\_status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_ALLOCATION\_FAILED}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Obtain pointers to the model\textquotesingle{}s input and output tensors */}
\NormalTok{  input }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter\_GetInputTensor}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
\NormalTok{  output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter\_GetOutputTensor}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter}\OperatorTok{,} \DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{);}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{input }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL }\OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ output }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_FAIL}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{/* C implementation for prediction */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_predict}\OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x\_val}\OperatorTok{,} \DataTypeTok{float}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ y\_val}\OperatorTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL }\OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ input }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL }\OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ output }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL }\OperatorTok{||}\NormalTok{ y\_val }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ NULL}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_INVALID\_STATE}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Set the input tensor data */}
\NormalTok{  input}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_val}\OperatorTok{;}

  \CommentTok{/* Run inference */}
\NormalTok{  TfLiteStatus invoke\_status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TF\_MicroInterpreter\_Invoke}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{interpreter}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{invoke\_status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ kTfLiteOk}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_FAIL}\OperatorTok{;}
  \OperatorTok{\}}

  \CommentTok{/* Get the output value */}
  \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{y\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ output}\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{data}\OperatorTok{.}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{[}\DecValTok{0}\OperatorTok{];}
  
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Modify your \texttt{app.c} to use this component:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\PreprocessorTok{\#include }\ImportTok{"sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor.h"}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_init() */}
\NormalTok{sl\_status\_t status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_init}\OperatorTok{();}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Failed to initialize TinyML model: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{status}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{/* In app\_process\_action() */}
\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ x\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ position }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ X\_RANGE}\OperatorTok{;}
\DataTypeTok{float}\NormalTok{ y\_val }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}\BuiltInTok{f}\OperatorTok{;}

\CommentTok{/* Run inference */}
\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sl\_tflite\_sine\_predictor\_predict}\OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{x\_val}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{\&}\NormalTok{y\_val}\OperatorTok{);}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \OperatorTok{(}\NormalTok{status }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ SL\_STATUS\_OK}\OperatorTok{)} \OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{  printf}\OperatorTok{(}\StringTok{"Inference failed: }\SpecialCharTok{\%d\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\OperatorTok{,} \OperatorTok{(}\DataTypeTok{int}\OperatorTok{)}\NormalTok{status}\OperatorTok{);}
  \ControlFlowTok{return}\OperatorTok{;}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This approach encapsulates the TensorFlow Lite components behind a C
API, making it easier to use throughout your application.

\section{Conclusion}\label{conclusion-2}

In this chapter, we've built a complete TinyML application for the
EFR32MG24 using the Gecko SDK and Simplicity Studio. This approach
simplifies deployment by leveraging the hardware abstraction layer and
pre-integrated components of the SDK.

Key takeaways from this implementation include:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Using Simplicity Studio's project templates to quickly set up a TinyML
  environment
\item
  Integrating a pre-trained TensorFlow Lite model with the application
\item
  Visualizing model predictions through LED brightness or LCD displays
\item
  Applying memory and power optimizations
\item
  Measuring and improving performance
\item
  Creating a reusable component for TinyML functionality
\end{enumerate}

This ``Hello World'' example serves as a foundation for more complex
TinyML applications on the EFR32 platform. From here, you can experiment
with:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  More sophisticated models like keyword spotting, gesture recognition,
  or anomaly detection
\item
  Sensor integration for real-time data collection
\item
  Custom hardware interfaces for different output methods
\item
  Multi-model systems that combine different ML capabilities
\end{itemize}

The Gecko SDK approach makes these extensions more accessible by
providing a structured and optimized framework specifically designed for
Silicon Labs devices.

\chapter{Handwriting Digit
Recognition}\label{handwriting-digit-recognition}

\section{Chapter Objectives}\label{chapter-objectives}

• Develop a CNN model for handwriting recognition using the MNIST
dataset

• Optimize the model through quantization to fit within microcontroller
constraints

• Implement the model on the EFR32xG24 platform

• Evaluate performance metrics including accuracy, model size, and
inference time

• Identify practical considerations and optimization strategies for
TinyML deployment

\section{Overview}\label{overview-1}

This chapter presents the implementation and evaluation of a handwriting
recognition system on the Silicon Labs EFR32xG24 microcontroller, a
resource-constrained device designed for edge computing applications.
The research demonstrates how Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) can
be effectively deployed for on-device inference despite significant
memory and processing limitations. The methodology encompasses model
development using TensorFlow, optimization through quantization
techniques, and deployment on embedded hardware. The implemented system
achieves 99.18\% accuracy on the MNIST dataset while maintaining a model
size of approximately 101.59 KB, representing a 91\% reduction from the
unoptimized model. This work illustrates the feasibility of deploying
sophisticated machine learning applications directly on edge devices,
enabling privacy-preserving, low-latency inference for applications
ranging from smart interfaces to IoT sensing. The chapter details the
technical challenges encountered during implementation and discusses
optimization strategies relevant to TinyML deployment on
microcontroller-class devices.

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

The intersection of artificial intelligence and edge computing has given
rise to a new paradigm for deploying machine learning models directly on
resource-constrained devices. This approach, commonly referred to as
TinyML, enables on-device inference without requiring cloud
connectivity, offering advantages in privacy, latency, and power
efficiency. By processing data locally, edge AI solutions eliminate the
need to transmit potentially sensitive information to remote servers,
reduce response times by avoiding network round-trips, and minimize
energy consumption associated with wireless communication.

Handwriting recognition represents an ideal test case for edge AI
deployment. As a classical pattern recognition problem, it demonstrates
the capabilities of machine learning while remaining sufficiently
bounded in scope to fit within the constraints of microcontroller-based
systems. When successfully implemented on edge devices, handwriting
recognition can enable various applications, from smart note-taking
tools to authentication systems, operating independently from cloud
infrastructure.

\subsection{Challenges of Microcontroller
Deployment}\label{challenges-of-microcontroller-deployment}

Deploying neural networks on microcontrollers presents significant
technical challenges due to their limited computational resources. The
EFR32xG24 microcontroller used in this chapter, while relatively
advanced for its class, operates with strict constraints. The processing
power is limited to a 78 MHz ARM Cortex-M33 processor, with memory
capacity of only 256 KB RAM and 1536 KB flash storage, and a minimal
power budget for battery-operated scenarios. These limitations
necessitate careful optimization of model architecture, quantization
strategies, and memory management techniques. Standard machine learning
frameworks and models designed for server or mobile deployment are
typically orders of magnitude too large for microcontroller
environments, requiring substantial adaptation.

\subsection{Chapter Objectives}\label{chapter-objectives-1}

This chapter aims to develop a CNN model for handwriting recognition
using the MNIST dataset and optimize it through quantization to fit
within microcontroller constraints. It demonstrates the implementation
of the model on the EFR32xG24 platform and evaluates the performance
metrics including accuracy, model size, and inference time.
Additionally, it identifies practical considerations and optimization
strategies for TinyML deployment, providing valuable insights for
researchers and practitioners in this emerging field.

\section{Background \& Related Work}\label{background-related-work}

\subsection{TinyML: Machine Learning for Embedded
Systems}\label{tinyml-machine-learning-for-embedded-systems}

TinyML represents the field of machine learning tailored specifically
for extremely resource-constrained devices. Unlike traditional deep
learning models that may require gigabytes of memory and powerful GPUs,
TinyML models typically occupy kilobytes of storage and run on
microcontrollers with limited computational capabilities. This
significant reduction in resource requirements is achieved through
specialized model architectures, parameter optimization, and
quantization techniques.

The development of TensorFlow Lite for Microcontrollers has been
instrumental in advancing TinyML applications. This framework provides
an optimized runtime for executing neural network models on devices with
as little as 16 KB of RAM, enabling a wide range of on-device inference
capabilities. Recent research has demonstrated successful TinyML
implementations for applications including wake word detection, anomaly
detection, and gesture recognition. The work of Warden and Situnayake
(2020) has been particularly influential in establishing methodologies
for deploying machine learning models on ultra-low-power
microcontrollers.

\subsection{Convolutional Neural Networks for Image
Recognition}\label{convolutional-neural-networks-for-image-recognition}

CNNs have revolutionized computer vision tasks through their ability to
automatically extract hierarchical features from image data. The
architecture of CNNs, inspired by the visual cortex of mammals, employs
convolutional layers that apply spatial filters to input data, capturing
patterns at different scales and abstraction levels.

For handwriting recognition, CNNs offer significant advantages over
traditional machine learning approaches. Their translation invariance
property---achieved through convolutional operations and pooling
layers---allows them to recognize digits regardless of their precise
position within the input image. This characteristic is particularly
valuable for handwriting recognition, where variations in style,
position, and scale are common.

The MNIST dataset (LeCun et al., 1998) has become the standard benchmark
for handwriting recognition algorithms. Comprising 60,000 training
images and 10,000 test images of handwritten digits (0-9), each
normalized to 28×28 pixels, this dataset provides a consistent
evaluation framework for comparing different approaches. Its widespread
adoption has facilitated meaningful comparisons across diverse
algorithmic strategies and implementation approaches.

\subsection{Model Optimization for Resource-Constrained
Devices}\label{model-optimization-for-resource-constrained-devices}

Deploying neural networks on microcontrollers requires substantial
optimization to fit within memory constraints while maintaining
acceptable inference performance. Several key techniques have emerged in
this domain. Quantization involves converting floating-point weights and
activations to lower-precision formats (e.g., 8-bit integers), which
reduces memory requirements and improves computational efficiency.
Post-training quantization can reduce model size by up to 75\% with
minimal accuracy loss, making it a crucial technique for microcontroller
deployment.

Model architecture selection also plays a critical role in TinyML
applications. Lightweight architectures like MobileNet or SqueezeNet
prioritize parameter efficiency, achieving competitive accuracy with
significantly fewer parameters than traditional models. These
architectures incorporate depthwise separable convolutions and other
parameter-efficient operations specifically designed for
resource-constrained environments.

Pruning represents another effective optimization strategy, involving
the systematic removal of redundant or less important connections within
a network to reduce model size while preserving most of the original
accuracy. Knowledge distillation, where a compact ``student'' model is
trained to replicate the behavior of a larger ``teacher'' model, can
also produce efficient networks suitable for embedded deployment.

Recent work by Banbury et al.~(2021) has focused on benchmarking TinyML
systems, highlighting the trade-offs between model size, accuracy, and
inference latency across different optimization approaches and hardware
platforms. These benchmarks provide valuable insights for selecting
appropriate optimization strategies based on specific application
requirements and hardware constraints.

\section{Methodology}\label{methodology}

\subsection{System Architecture}\label{system-architecture}

The handwriting recognition system employs a modular architecture
engineered for efficient operation within the constraints of the
microcontroller platform. At its core, the system processes 28×28 pixel
grayscale images through a series of specialized components working in
concert.

Central to the system's operation is the TensorFlow Lite Runtime, which
orchestrates the execution of the quantized CNN model. This component
manages the complex tasks of memory allocation and operation scheduling,
ensuring efficient use of the limited computational resources.
Surrounding this runtime is a carefully sized tensor arena---a dedicated
70KB memory buffer that serves as working space for tensors during the
inference process.

The input processing module transforms raw image data, whether from
predefined test arrays or external sources, into the appropriate format
for neural network inference. Following model execution, the
classification output component analyzes probability distributions to
determine the recognized digit and its associated confidence score.
Results flow through a communication interface utilizing USART or EUSART
protocols, enabling external monitoring and system evaluation.

Through strategic partitioning of responsibilities, this architecture
maximizes the capabilities of the EFR32xG24 while maintaining the
flexibility needed for potential future enhancements. Each component can
be optimized independently, allowing for targeted improvements without
necessitating wholesale system redesign.

\subsection{Model Design and Training}\label{model-design-and-training}

\subsubsection{Dataset Preparation}\label{dataset-preparation}

The MNIST dataset was used for model training and evaluation. The
dataset consists of 70,000 handwritten digit images (60,000 for
training, 10,000 for testing), each normalized to 28×28 pixels in
grayscale format. Prior to training, preprocessing steps were applied,
including reshaping the images to include a channel dimension (28×28×1),
normalizing pixel values to the range {[}0, 1{]}, and ensuring
consistent data types for training stability. The following code
illustrates the preprocessing procedure:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Load dataset}
\NormalTok{(train\_images, train\_labels), (test\_images, test\_labels) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mnist.load\_data()}

\CommentTok{\# Reshape and normalize}
\NormalTok{train\_images }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ train\_images.reshape((}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)).astype(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}float32\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{255.0}
\NormalTok{test\_images }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ test\_images.reshape((}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)).astype(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}float32\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{255.0}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{CNN Architecture}\label{cnn-architecture}

The model architecture was designed to balance accuracy with parameter
efficiency, a critical consideration for microcontroller deployment. The
network consists of three convolutional blocks followed by fully
connected layers, as shown in Table 1.

\textbf{Table 1: CNN Model Architecture}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Layer Type & Parameters & Output Shape \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Input & - & (28, 28, 1) \\
Conv2D & 3×3, 32 filters, ReLU & 320 \\
MaxPooling2D & 2×2 & 0 \\
Conv2D & 3×3, 64 filters, ReLU & 18,496 \\
MaxPooling2D & 2×2 & 0 \\
Conv2D & 3×3, 64 filters, ReLU & 36,928 \\
Flatten & - & 0 \\
Dense & 64 neurons, ReLU & 36,928 \\
Dense & 10 neurons, Softmax & 650 \\
\end{longtable}

The model was implemented using TensorFlow's Keras API:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ models.Sequential([}
\NormalTok{    layers.Conv2D(}\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, (}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, input\_shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{28}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
\NormalTok{    layers.MaxPooling2D((}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)),}
\NormalTok{    layers.Conv2D(}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, (}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    layers.MaxPooling2D((}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)),}
\NormalTok{    layers.Conv2D(}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, (}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    layers.Flatten(),}
\NormalTok{    layers.Dense(}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}relu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    layers.Dense(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, activation}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}softmax\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{Training Configuration}\label{training-configuration}

The model was trained with the following configurations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{model.}\BuiltInTok{compile}\NormalTok{(optimizer}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}adam\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{              loss}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sparse\_categorical\_crossentropy\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{              metrics}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}accuracy\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{])}

\NormalTok{model.fit(train\_images, train\_labels, epochs}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Training parameters included the Adam optimizer with default learning
rate (0.001), sparse categorical cross-entropy loss function, accuracy
metrics, 5 epochs, and default batch size (32). The relatively small
number of epochs was sufficient due to the simplicity of the MNIST
dataset and the model's efficient learning capacity. Training was
performed in Google Colab to leverage GPU acceleration.

\subsection{Model Optimization}\label{model-optimization}

\subsubsection{Post-Training
Quantization}\label{post-training-quantization}

To meet the memory constraints of the EFR32xG24 microcontroller, the
trained model was subjected to post-training quantization using
TensorFlow Lite's quantization framework. This process converted the
32-bit floating-point weights and activations to 8-bit integers,
significantly reducing the model size while preserving accuracy.

The quantization process required defining a representative dataset to
calibrate the dynamic range of activations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ representative\_data\_gen():}
    \CommentTok{"""Generator function for a representative dataset for quantization."""}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ input\_value }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ tf.data.Dataset.from\_tensor\_slices(train\_images).batch(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{).take(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{):}
        \ControlFlowTok{yield}\NormalTok{ [tf.cast(input\_value, tf.float32)]}

\CommentTok{\# Configure the converter for full integer quantization}
\NormalTok{converter }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.lite.TFLiteConverter.from\_keras\_model(model)}
\NormalTok{converter.optimizations }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [tf.lite.Optimize.DEFAULT]}
\NormalTok{converter.representative\_dataset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ representative\_data\_gen}
\NormalTok{converter.target\_spec.supported\_ops }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [tf.lite.OpsSet.TFLITE\_BUILTINS\_INT8]}
\NormalTok{converter.inference\_input\_type }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.int8}
\NormalTok{converter.inference\_output\_type }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tf.int8}

\CommentTok{\# Convert and save the model}
\NormalTok{quantized\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ converter.convert()}
\ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"hw\_model.tflite"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"wb"}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{    f.write(quantized\_model)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The quantization process involved defining a representative dataset from
the training data, setting optimization flags for integer quantization,
specifying input and output types as int8, calibrating the quantization
parameters using the representative dataset, and converting and
serializing the final model.

\subsubsection{Model Verification}\label{model-verification}

After quantization, the model was verified to ensure that the accuracy
remained acceptable. This verification process involved loading the
quantized model with the TensorFlow Lite interpreter, running inference
on the test set, comparing the accuracy against the original
floating-point model, and analyzing the confusion matrix to identify any
systematic errors introduced by quantization. The results confirmed that
the quantization process preserved the high accuracy of the original
model while dramatically reducing its size.

\subsection{Embedded Implementation}\label{embedded-implementation}

The embedded implementation utilized Silicon Labs' Simplicity Studio
development environment. The implementation process followed a
systematic approach, beginning with the creation of a new C++ project
and the addition of the TensorFlow Lite Micro component through the
Component Library. The tensor arena size and I/O interfaces were
carefully configured based on the model's requirements, and the
quantized model was integrated into the project. Finally, the inference
pipeline was implemented to handle the end-to-end process from input
acquisition to result communication.

Memory management represented a critical aspect of the implementation
due to the constraints of the microcontroller. The tensor arena was
configured to 70KB based on extensive profiling of the model's
operational memory footprint. The profiling process involved
instrumenting the model execution to track maximum memory usage across
various input samples, with particular attention to intermediate tensor
allocations during critical network layers such as the larger
convolutional operations. This methodical approach ensured sufficient
working space for inference while optimizing RAM utilization. All
buffers were statically allocated to avoid heap fragmentation, which can
be particularly problematic in long-running embedded applications. Input
and output tensors were structured to minimize memory copying
operations, reducing both the memory footprint and computational
overhead.

The inference pipeline was implemented in C++ and consisted of several
key steps. The initialization phase involved loading the model and
allocating tensors, establishing the foundation for subsequent inference
operations. Input processing handled the reading of input images, either
from predefined arrays or external sources, and prepared them for model
execution. The model execution phase utilized the TensorFlow Lite Micro
interpreter to run inference on the prepared input, while output
processing determined the predicted digit based on the model's output
probabilities. Finally, the result communication phase transmitted the
recognition results via the UART interface, enabling external monitoring
and evaluation of the system's performance.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Simplified code snippet showing the key inference components}
